That’s where it really shows up. If a manager questions one of my projections, I immediately hear it as proof that I’m slipping, that I’m not as sharp as I used to be, and that everyone can see I’m failing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're failing or not as capable in other areas of your life, outside of work?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way. It’s mostly work where I feel that. Like if a manager questions one of my projections, I just immediately take it as confirmation that I’m not as sharp as I used to be, and that everyone can see I’m failing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of not being as sharp as you used to be at work is related to a fear of failing in general?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. At work, if a manager questions one of my projections, I don’t just hear it as a normal question. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I’m just not sleeping in any useful way anymore. I’ll lie there awake replaying client models in my head, and then I wake up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach tight. By the time I go to work, I feel like I never actually rested.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing stress or anxiety outside of work as well?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I notice it most around work, honestly. Outside of that, I guess I feel tense sometimes, but I haven’t really separated it out in my head.\n\nDoctor: Are your sleeping problems and feelings of tension only happening on workdays?\n\nPatient: No, not only workdays. It’s worse when I’m thinking about client models, but even when I’m not going in, I still don’t really sleep well. I’ll wake up around 3 or 4 with my stomach tight, and it feels like I never got real rest.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the client models that's causing you stress?\n\nPatient: It’s less one specific model and more that I can’t think through them the way I used to. The spreadsheets used to be second nature, but now I lose the thread, reread the same assumptions over and over, and it takes me hours. Then before a presentation I’m still worried I’ve missed something obvious.\n\nDoctor: Is the stomach tightness you experience in the morning related to thoughts about work or something else?\n\nPatient: Mostly work thoughts, I think. I’ll lie there replaying the client models in my head, worrying through assumptions or whether I missed something. Then I wake up at 3 or 4 with my stomach tight, and by the time I go in, it feels like I never really slept.\n\nDoctor: Is the difficulty sleeping only happening when you have a big project or presentation coming up at work?\n\nPatient: No, it’s not just when there’s a big presentation. Those make it worse, but at this point I barely sleep in any useful way most nights. I’ll lie there going over client models in my head, then wake up around 3 or 4 with my stomach tight, and I go to work feeling like I never actually rested.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that you are afraid will happen if you don't get the client models right?\n\nPatient: I’m afraid it’ll prove what I already worry about—that I’m not as sharp as I used to be. If a manager questions one of my projections, even if it’s a normal question, I immediately take it as everyone seeing that I’m failing.\n\nDoctor: Is this fear of not being as sharp as you used to be something that you worry about in areas of your life outside of work?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly at work. That’s where it really shows up. If a manager questions one of my projections, I immediately hear it as proof that I’m slipping, that I’m not as sharp as I used to be, and that everyone can see I’m failing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're failing or not as capable in other areas of your life, outside of work?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way. It’s mostly work where I feel that. Like if a manager questions one of my projections, I just immediately take it as confirmation that I’m not as sharp as I used to be, and that everyone can see I’m failing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of not being as sharp as you used to be at work is related to a fear of failing in general?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. At work, if a manager questions one of my projections, I don’t just hear it as a normal question. I take it as confirmation that I’m not as sharp as I used to be, and that everyone can see I’m failing.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear failing in situations outside of work, like in personal relationships or hobbies?\n\nPatient: Not so much in personal relationships or hobbies. It’s really tied to work for me. When a manager questions one of my projections, I immediately take it as a sign that I’m not as sharp as I used to be, and that people can see I’m failing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry about failing in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Not really. I don’t usually worry about failing in personal relationships. It’s more at work—like when a manager questions one of my projections, I take it as proof that I’m not as sharp as I used to be, and that everyone can see I’m failing.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of failing something you've experienced before in your life, outside of your current work situation?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I can’t really point to a time outside of this work situation where it felt the same. It’s possible I’ve had moments like that, but nothing stands out clearly to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel this fear of failing if you were to change jobs or leave your current work environment?\n\nPatient: I think it might depend on the job, but honestly, it feels like it could follow me. If someone in a new job questioned my projections or my work, I’d probably still take it as confirmation that I’m not as sharp as I used to be, and that people can see I’m failing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would worry about not being sharp or capable in a situation where you are not being evaluated or judged by others, such as a hobby?\n\nPatient: Probably less so, if no one was judging it. The sharpness thing really comes up when someone questions my work. Like if a manager challenges one of my projections, I immediately take it as proof that I’m not capable anymore, and that everyone can see I’m failing. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F51.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I’m just not sleeping in any useful way anymore. I’ll lie there awake replaying client models in my head, and then I wake up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach tight. By the time I go to work, I feel like I never actually rested.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing stress or anxiety outside of work as well?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I notice it most around work, honestly. Outside of that, I guess I feel tense sometimes, but I haven’t really separated it out in my head.\n\nDoctor: Are your sleeping problems and feelings of tension only happening on workdays?\n\nPatient: No, not only workdays. It’s worse when I’m thinking about client models, but even when I’m not going in, I still don’t really sleep well. I’ll wake up around 3 or 4 with my stomach tight, and it feels like I never got real rest.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the client models that's causing you stress?\n\nPatient: It’s less one specific model and more that I can’t think through them the way I used to. The spreadsheets used to be second nature, but now I lose the thread, reread the same assumptions over and over, and it takes me hours. Then before a presentation I’m still worried I’ve missed something obvious.\n\nDoctor: Is the stomach tightness you experience in the morning related to thoughts about work or something else?\n\nPatient: Mostly work thoughts, I think. I’ll lie there replaying the client models in my head, worrying through assumptions or whether I missed something. Then I wake up at 3 or 4 with my stomach tight, and by the time I go in, it feels like I never really slept.\n\nDoctor: Is the difficulty sleeping only happening when you have a big project or presentation coming up at work?\n\nPatient: No, it’s not just when there’s a big presentation. Those make it worse, but at this point I barely sleep in any useful way most nights. I’ll lie there going over client models in my head, then wake up around 3 or 4 with my stomach tight, and I go to work feeling like I never actually rested.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that you are afraid will happen if you don't get the client models right?\n\nPatient: I’m afraid it’ll prove what I already worry about—that I’m not as sharp as I used to be. If a manager questions one of my projections, even if it’s a normal question, I immediately take it as everyone seeing that I’m failing.\n\nDoctor: Is this fear of not being as sharp as you used to be something that you worry about in areas of your life outside of work?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly at work. That’s where it really shows up. If a manager questions one of my projections, I immediately hear it as proof that I’m slipping, that I’m not as sharp as I used to be, and that everyone can see I’m failing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're failing or not as capable in other areas of your life, outside of work?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way. It’s mostly work where I feel that. Like if a manager questions one of my projections, I just immediately take it as confirmation that I’m not as sharp as I used to be, and that everyone can see I’m failing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of not being as sharp as you used to be at work is related to a fear of failing in general?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. At work, if a manager questions one of my projections, I don’t just hear it as a normal question. I take it as confirmation that I’m not as sharp as I used to be, and that everyone can see I’m failing.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear failing in situations outside of work, like in personal relationships or hobbies?\n\nPatient: Not so much in personal relationships or hobbies. It’s really tied to work for me. When a manager questions one of my projections, I immediately take it as a sign that I’m not as sharp as I used to be, and that people can see I’m failing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry about failing in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Not really. I don’t usually worry about failing in personal relationships. It’s more at work—like when a manager questions one of my projections, I take it as proof that I’m not as sharp as I used to be, and that everyone can see I’m failing.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of failing something you've experienced before in your life, outside of your current work situation?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I can’t really point to a time outside of this work situation where it felt the same. It’s possible I’ve had moments like that, but nothing stands out clearly to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel this fear of failing if you were to change jobs or leave your current work environment?\n\nPatient: I think it might depend on the job, but honestly, it feels like it could follow me. If someone in a new job questioned my projections or my work, I’d probably still take it as confirmation that I’m not as sharp as I used to be, and that people can see I’m failing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would worry about not being sharp or capable in a situation where you are not being evaluated or judged by others, such as a hobby?\n\nPatient: Probably less so, if no one was judging it. The sharpness thing really comes up when someone questions my work. Like if a manager challenges one of my projections, I immediately take it as proof that I’m not capable anymore, and that everyone can see I’m failing. With a hobby, I’m not sure it would hit the same way unless I felt someone was evaluating me.\n\nDoctor: Would you worry about not being sharp or capable if you were doing something you've always been good at, outside of work?\n\nPatient: I think if it was really outside of work and no one was weighing in, maybe not as much. But if someone questioned me, even in something I’m usually good at, I think I’d still hear it as, “You’re not sharp anymore.” That’s where my mind goes pretty quickly—like they can see I’m failing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If a manager questions one of my projections, I immediately hear it as proof that I’m slipping, that I’m not as sharp as I used to be, and that everyone can see I’m failing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're failing or not as capable in other areas of your life, outside of work?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way. It’s mostly work where I feel that. Like if a manager questions one of my projections, I just immediately take it as confirmation that I’m not as sharp as I used to be, and that everyone can see I’m failing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of not being as sharp as you used to be at work is related to a fear of failing in general?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. At work, if a manager questions one of my projections, I don’t just hear it as a normal question. I take it as confirmation that I’m not as sharp as I used to be, and that everyone can see I’m failing.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear failing in situations outside of work, like in personal relationships or hobbies?\n\nPatient: Not so much in personal relationships or hobbies. It’s really tied to work for me. When a manager questions one of my projections, I immediately take it as a sign that I’m not as sharp as I used to be, and that people can see I’m failing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry about failing in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Not really. I don’t usually worry about failing in personal relationships. It’s more at work—like when a manager questions one of my projections, I take it as proof that I’m not as sharp as I used to be, and that everyone can see I’m failing.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of failing something you've experienced before in your life, outside of your current work situation?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I can’t really point to a time outside of this work situation where it felt the same. It’s possible I’ve had moments like that, but nothing stands out clearly to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel this fear of failing if you were to change jobs or leave your current work environment?\n\nPatient: I think it might depend on the job, but honestly, it feels like it could follow me. If someone in a new job questioned my projections or my work, I’d probably still take it as confirmation that I’m not as sharp as I used to be, and that people can see I’m failing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would worry about not being sharp or capable in a situation where you are not being evaluated or judged by others, such as a hobby?\n\nPatient: Probably less so, if no one was judging it. The sharpness thing really comes up when someone questions my work. Like if a manager challenges one of my projections, I immediately take it as proof that I’m not capable anymore, and that everyone can see I’m failing. With a hobby, I’m not sure it would hit the same way unless I felt someone was evaluating me.\n\nDoctor: Would you worry about not being sharp or capable if you were doing something you've always been good at, outside of work?\n\nPatient: I think if it was really outside of work and no one was weighing in, maybe not as much. But if someone questioned me, even in something I’m usually good at, I think I’d still hear it as, “You’re not sharp anymore.” That’s where my mind goes pretty quickly—like they can see I’m failing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel like a failure if you made a mistake in a non-work-related activity?\n\nPatient: Maybe, but I think it would be much stronger if someone else noticed or questioned it. Like at work, when a manager questions one of my projections, I immediately hear it as proof that I’m not as sharp as I used to be, and that everyone can see I’m failing. If a manager questions one of my projections, even if it’s a normal question, I immediately take it as everyone seeing that I’m failing.\n\nDoctor: Is this fear of not being as sharp as you used to be something that you worry about in areas of your life outside of work?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly at work. If someone in a new job questioned my projections or my work, I’d probably still take it as confirmation that I’m not as sharp as I used to be, and that people can see I’m failing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would worry about not being sharp or capable in a situation where you are not being evaluated or judged by others, such as a hobby?\n\nPatient: Probably less so, if no one was judging it. The sharpness thing really comes up when someone questions my work. Like if a manager challenges one of my projections, I immediately take it as proof that I’m not capable anymore, and that everyone can see I’m failing. With a hobby, I’m not sure it would hit the same way unless I felt someone was evaluating me.\n\nDoctor: Would you worry about not being sharp or capable if you were doing something you've always been good at, outside of work?\n\nPatient: I think if it was really outside of work and no one was weighing in, maybe not as much. But if someone questioned me, even in something I’m usually good at, I think I’d still hear it as, “You’re not sharp anymore.” That’s where my mind goes pretty quickly—like they can see I’m failing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel like a failure if you made a mistake in a non-work-related activity?\n\nPatient: Maybe, but I think it would be much stronger if someone else noticed or questioned it. So even outside work, if I made a mistake and someone pointed it out, I could see myself taking it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel like you're failing if you made a mistake at work and nobody else knew about it?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I would. Even if nobody else knew, I’d still probably take it as a sign that I’m not as sharp as I used to be. Like if a manager questions one of my projections, I just immediately take it as confirmation that I’m not as sharp as I used to be, and that everyone can see I’m failing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of not being as sharp as you used to be at work is related to a fear of failing in general?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. At work, if a manager questions one of my projections, I don’t just hear it as a normal question. I take it as confirmation that I’m not as sharp as I used to be, and that everyone can see I’m failing.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear failing in situations outside of work, like in personal relationships or hobbies?\n\nPatient: Not so much in personal relationships or hobbies. It’s really tied to work for me. When a manager questions one of my projections, I immediately take it as a sign that I’m not as sharp as I used to be, and that people can see I’m failing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry about failing in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Not really. I don’t usually worry about failing in personal relationships. It’s more at work—like when a manager questions one of my projections, I take it as proof that I’m not as sharp as I used to be, and that everyone can see I’m failing.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of failing something you've experienced before in your life, outside of your current work situation?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I can’t really point to a time outside of this work situation where it felt the same. It’s possible I’ve had moments like that, but nothing stands out clearly to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel this fear of failing if you were to change jobs or leave your current work environment?\n\nPatient: I think it might depend on the job, but honestly, it feels like it could follow me. If someone in a new job questioned my projections or my work, I’d probably still take it as confirmation that I’m not as sharp as I used to be, and that people can see I’m failing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would worry about not being sharp or capable in a situation where you are not being evaluated or judged by others, such as a hobby?\n\nPatient: Probably less so, if no one was judging it. The sharpness thing really comes up when someone questions my work. Like if a manager challenges one of my projections, I immediately take it as proof that I’m not capable anymore, and that everyone can see I’m failing. With a hobby, I’m not sure it would hit the same way unless I felt someone was evaluating me.\n\nDoctor: Would you worry about not being sharp or capable if you were doing something you've always been good at, outside of work?\n\nPatient: I think if it was really outside of work and no one was weighing in, maybe not as much. But if someone questioned me, even in something I’m usually good at, I think I’d still hear it as, “You’re not sharp anymore.” That’s where my mind goes pretty quickly—like they can see I’m failing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel like a failure if you made a mistake in a non-work-related activity?\n\nPatient: Maybe, but I think it would be much stronger if someone else noticed or questioned it. Like at work, when a manager questions one of my projections, I immediately hear it as proof that I’m not as sharp as I used to be, and that everyone can see I’m failing. So even outside work, if I made a mistake and someone pointed it out, I could see myself taking it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel like you're failing if you made a mistake at work and nobody else knew about it?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I would. Even if nobody else knew, I’d still probably take it as a sign that I’m not as sharp as I used to be. But it gets much worse when someone else notices or questions it—like when a manager challenges one of my projections, I immediately feel like that confirms I’m failing and everyone can see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worth as a person is tied to your performance at work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, more than I’d like to admit. When a manager questions one of my projections, I don’t just hear it as a normal question. I immediately take it as confirmation that I’m not as sharp as I used to be, and that everyone can see I’m failing. So, yeah, my sense of worth does feel tied up in how I’m doing at work.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It’s more at work—like when a manager questions one of my projections, I take it as proof that I’m not as sharp as I used to be, and that everyone can see I’m failing.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of failing something you've experienced before in your life, outside of your current work situation?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I can’t really point to a time outside of this work situation where it felt the same. It’s possible I’ve had moments like that, but nothing stands out clearly to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel this fear of failing if you were to change jobs or leave your current work environment?\n\nPatient: I think it might depend on the job, but honestly, it feels like it could follow me. If someone in a new job questioned my projections or my work, I’d probably still take it as confirmation that I’m not as sharp as I used to be, and that people can see I’m failing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would worry about not being sharp or capable in a situation where you are not being evaluated or judged by others, such as a hobby?\n\nPatient: Probably less so, if no one was judging it. The sharpness thing really comes up when someone questions my work. Like if a manager challenges one of my projections, I immediately take it as proof that I’m not capable anymore, and that everyone can see I’m failing. With a hobby, I’m not sure it would hit the same way unless I felt someone was evaluating me.\n\nDoctor: Would you worry about not being sharp or capable if you were doing something you've always been good at, outside of work?\n\nPatient: I think if it was really outside of work and no one was weighing in, maybe not as much. But if someone questioned me, even in something I’m usually good at, I think I’d still hear it as, “You’re not sharp anymore.” That’s where my mind goes pretty quickly—like they can see I’m failing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel like a failure if you made a mistake in a non-work-related activity?\n\nPatient: Maybe, but I think it would be much stronger if someone else noticed or questioned it. Like at work, when a manager questions one of my projections, I immediately hear it as proof that I’m not as sharp as I used to be, and that everyone can see I’m failing. So even outside work, if I made a mistake and someone pointed it out, I could see myself taking it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel like you're failing if you made a mistake at work and nobody else knew about it?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I would. Even if nobody else knew, I’d still probably take it as a sign that I’m not as sharp as I used to be. But it gets much worse when someone else notices or questions it—like when a manager challenges one of my projections, I immediately feel like that confirms I’m failing and everyone can see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worth as a person is tied to your performance at work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, more than I’d like to admit. When a manager questions one of my projections, I don’t just hear it as a normal question. I immediately take it as confirmation that I’m not as sharp as I used to be, and that everyone can see I’m failing. So, yeah, my sense of worth does feel tied up in how I’m doing at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would be a failure as a person if you failed at your job?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s how it feels, honestly. If I failed at my job, I think I’d take it as more than just something going wrong at work. Even now, when a manager questions one of my projections, I immediately hear it as proof that I’m not sharp enough anymore, and that everyone can see I’m failing. So yes, I think I would feel like a failure as a person."
